    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Well, I guess what brings me in is... it's about my eating. Once I start eating these comfort foods, I just... I tell myself, 'Maria, that is enough.' But my hand still goes back to the pot. It is like I just can't make myself stop, no matter what I tell my brain.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating these comfort foods?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Afterward, I just... I feel so ashamed and sad about it. You know, making that food, it used to mean family and caring for my children. But now... now it feels like I've turned it into something bad. It's become something I feel like I have to hide from the people at my church.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these comfort foods when you're feeling stressed or emotional?\n\nPatient: I... I'm really not sure about that. I haven't paid much attention to whether I'm feeling stressed or upset when it happens. Sometimes I just start eating and... well, I honestly don't know if my emotions have anything to do with it. I just haven't noticed a pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you often, or was it just a one-time thing?\n\nPatient: No, it's not just a one-time thing. It's... well, since my friend passed, it's been happening most weeks. I'd say usually once or twice a week. It mostly seems to happen on those quiet afternoons, you know, after I get back from choir practice or when I'm done with my gardening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating these comfort foods makes you feel better while you're doing it?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean... I guess I haven't really thought about whether it makes me feel better in the moment. I'm not sure. I just know how terrible I feel afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat these comfort foods as a way to deal with difficult emotions or thoughts?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. Like I said before, I just haven't paid much attention to my thoughts or feelings when it happens. I don't know if I'm trying to deal with anything... I just find myself eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens when you're trying to distract yourself from thinking about your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say for sure. I mean, I miss her terribly, of course, but... I don't know if I'm doing it to distract myself. I just haven't noticed if she's on my mind right before I start eating. Like I said, I'm just not really paying attention to what's going through my head at the time. I just end up doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating these comfort foods is related to feeling sad about your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I really don't know. I mean, it's possible, I suppose, but I just haven't made that connection. Like I was saying, I'm just not really aware of what I'm feeling or why I'm doing it when it happens. I just know that I start eating and I can't seem to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens when you're feeling sad or upset about something?\n\nPatient: I know you keep asking me about my feelings, but I honestly just don't have a better answer for you. I'm not sitting there crying or feeling mad right before I go to the kitchen. At least, not that I notice. It just... happens. I wish I could tell you it was because I was upset about something, but I really just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be eating as a way to try and feel better, even if you're not really aware of it at the time?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I mean, I guess it's possible that I'm trying to make myself feel better without realizing it, but... I just don't know. I'm not thinking about cheering myself up or anything like that. I just find myself eating. I wish I had a clearer answer for you, I really do, but I just haven't noticed if that's why I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to eat these comfort foods, like you can't control it?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's exactly it. Once I start eating those comfort foods... I try to stop. I'll even tell myself, \"Maria, that is enough.\" But my hand just goes right back to the pot. It really feels like I just can't make myself stop, no matter what I tell myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've been eating these comfort foods for so long now that it's just become a habit for you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I mean... I haven't really thought about it like it's just some old habit. I don't know if I've been doing it so long that it's just automatic now. I just know what happens when I'm standing there with the food. I couldn't say for sure if it's just a habit or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat these comfort foods even if you weren't feeling any emotions at all?\n\nPatient: I honestly couldn't tell you. I've never really stopped to think about whether I'd still eat them if I wasn't feeling anything at all. It's... well, it's just hard to say. I really don't know the answer to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to eat these comfort foods if you were in a different environment or situation?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I haven't really been in a different situation lately, so it's hard to say. I mostly just stay home. I don't know if being somewhere else would change how I feel about eating those foods.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat these comfort foods if you were alone in a completely new place?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I haven't traveled or been to a new place in years, to be honest. I just don't know how I'd react or if I'd still reach for those same foods if I was sitting in a strange room somewhere. It's just hard to picture it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Well, I guess what brings me in is... it's about my eating. Once I start eating these comfort foods, I just... I tell myself, 'Maria, that is enough.' But my hand still goes back to the pot. It is like I just can't make myself stop, no matter what I tell my brain.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating these comfort foods?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Afterward, I just... I feel so ashamed and sad about it. You know, making that food, it used to mean family and caring for my children. But now... now it feels like I've turned it into something bad. It's become something I feel like I have to hide from the people at my church.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these comfort foods when you're feeling stressed or emotional?\n\nPatient: I... I'm really not sure about that. I haven't paid much attention to whether I'm feeling stressed or upset when it happens. Sometimes I just start eating and... well, I honestly don't know if my emotions have anything to do with it. I just haven't noticed a pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you often, or was it just a one-time thing?\n\nPatient: No, it's not just a one-time thing. It's... well, since my friend passed, it's been happening most weeks. I'd say usually once or twice a week. It mostly seems to happen on those quiet afternoons, you know, after I get back from choir practice or when I'm done with my gardening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating these comfort foods makes you feel better while you're doing it?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean... I guess I haven't really thought about whether it makes me feel better in the moment. I'm not sure. I just know how terrible I feel afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat these comfort foods as a way to deal with difficult emotions or thoughts?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. Like I said before, I just haven't paid much attention to my thoughts or feelings when it happens. I don't know if I'm trying to deal with anything... I just find myself eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens when you're trying to distract yourself from thinking about your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say for sure. I mean, I miss her terribly, of course, but... I don't know if I'm doing it to distract myself. I just haven't noticed if she's on my mind right before I start eating. Like I said, I'm just not really paying attention to what's going through my head at the time. I just end up doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating these comfort foods is related to feeling sad about your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I really don't know. I mean, it's possible, I suppose, but I just haven't made that connection. Like I was saying, I'm just not really aware of what I'm feeling or why I'm doing it when it happens. I just know that I start eating and I can't seem to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens when you're feeling sad or upset about something?\n\nPatient: I know you keep asking me about my feelings, but I honestly just don't have a better answer for you. I'm not sitting there crying or feeling mad right before I go to the kitchen. At least, not that I notice. It just... happens. I wish I could tell you it was because I was upset about something, but I really just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be eating as a way to try and feel better, even if you're not really aware of it at the time?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I mean, I guess it's possible that I'm trying to make myself feel better without realizing it, but... I just don't know. I'm not thinking about cheering myself up or anything like that. I just find myself eating. I wish I had a clearer answer for you, I really do, but I just haven't noticed if that's why I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to eat these comfort foods, like you can't control it?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's exactly it. Once I start eating those comfort foods... I try to stop. I'll even tell myself, \"Maria, that is enough.\" But my hand just goes right back to the pot. It really feels like I just can't make myself stop, no matter what I tell myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've been eating these comfort foods for so long now that it's just become a habit for you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I mean... I haven't really thought about it like it's just some old habit. I don't know if I've been doing it so long that it's just automatic now. I just know what happens when I'm standing there with the food. I couldn't say for sure if it's just a habit or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat these comfort foods even if you weren't feeling any emotions at all?\n\nPatient: I honestly couldn't tell you. I've never really stopped to think about whether I'd still eat them if I wasn't feeling anything at all. It's... well, it's just hard to say. I really don't know the answer to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to eat these comfort foods if you were in a different environment or situation?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I haven't really been in a different situation lately, so it's hard to say. I mostly just stay home. I don't know if being somewhere else would change how I feel about eating those foods.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat these comfort foods if you were alone in a completely new place?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I haven't traveled or been to a new place in years, to be honest. I just don't know how I'd react or if I'd still reach for those same foods if I was sitting in a strange room somewhere. It's just hard to picture it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you learned to eat these comfort foods as a way to cope with feelings or emotions from someone else, like your family or friends?\n\nPatient: I've never really thought about it like that. I don't know if I picked it up from anyone... my family, well, we just ate what we had. I don't remember anyone specifically eating to deal with their feelings. So, I guess I'm not really sure where it came from.\n\nDoctor: Did you grow up seeing family members or close relatives eat as a way to deal with their emotions?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying... no, I really didn't. When I was growing up, food was just food. We sat down and ate what was on the table for supper, and that was that. I never noticed my parents or anybody else doing that when they were sad or upset.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Well, I guess what brings me in is... it's about my eating. Once I start eating these comfort foods, I just... I tell myself, 'Maria, that is enough.' But my hand still goes back to the pot. It is like I just can't make myself stop, no matter what I tell my brain.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating these comfort foods?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Afterward, I just... I feel so ashamed and sad about it. You know, making that food, it used to mean family and caring for my children. But now... now it feels like I've turned it into something bad. It's become something I feel like I have to hide from the people at my church.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these comfort foods when you're feeling stressed or emotional?\n\nPatient: I... I'm really not sure about that. I haven't paid much attention to whether I'm feeling stressed or upset when it happens. Sometimes I just start eating and... well, I honestly don't know if my emotions have anything to do with it. I just haven't noticed a pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you often, or was it just a one-time thing?\n\nPatient: No, it's not just a one-time thing. It's... well, since my friend passed, it's been happening most weeks. I'd say usually once or twice a week. It mostly seems to happen on those quiet afternoons, you know, after I get back from choir practice or when I'm done with my gardening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating these comfort foods makes you feel better while you're doing it?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean... I guess I haven't really thought about whether it makes me feel better in the moment. I'm not sure. I just know how terrible I feel afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat these comfort foods as a way to deal with difficult emotions or thoughts?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. Like I said before, I just haven't paid much attention to my thoughts or feelings when it happens. I don't know if I'm trying to deal with anything... I just find myself eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens when you're trying to distract yourself from thinking about your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say for sure. I mean, I miss her terribly, of course, but... I don't know if I'm doing it to distract myself. I just haven't noticed if she's on my mind right before I start eating. Like I said, I'm just not really paying attention to what's going through my head at the time. I just end up doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating these comfort foods is related to feeling sad about your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I really don't know. I mean, it's possible, I suppose, but I just haven't made that connection. Like I was saying, I'm just not really aware of what I'm feeling or why I'm doing it when it happens. I just know that I start eating and I can't seem to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens when you're feeling sad or upset about something?\n\nPatient: I know you keep asking me about my feelings, but I honestly just don't have a better answer for you. I'm not sitting there crying or feeling mad right before I go to the kitchen. At least, not that I notice. It just... happens. I wish I could tell you it was because I was upset about something, but I really just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be eating as a way to try and feel better, even if you're not really aware of it at the time?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I mean, I guess it's possible that I'm trying to make myself feel better without realizing it, but... I just don't know. I'm not thinking about cheering myself up or anything like that. I just find myself eating. I wish I had a clearer answer for you, I really do, but I just haven't noticed if that's why I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to eat these comfort foods, like you can't control it?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's exactly it. Once I start eating those comfort foods... I try to stop. I'll even tell myself, \"Maria, that is enough.\" But my hand just goes right back to the pot. It really feels like I just can't make myself stop, no matter what I tell myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've been eating these comfort foods for so long now that it's just become a habit for you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I mean... I haven't really thought about it like it's just some old habit. I don't know if I've been doing it so long that it's just automatic now. I just know what happens when I'm standing there with the food. I've never really stopped to think about whether I'd still eat them if I wasn't feeling anything at all. It's... well, it's just hard to say. I really don't know the answer to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to eat these comfort foods if you were in a different environment or situation?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I haven't really been in a different situation lately, so it's hard to say. I mostly just stay home. I don't know if being somewhere else would change how I feel about eating those foods.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat these comfort foods if you were alone in a completely new place?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I haven't traveled or been to a new place in years, to be honest. I just don't know how I'd react or if I'd still reach for those same foods if I was sitting in a strange room somewhere. Like I said, food wasn't really a big emotional thing for us. We just ate our meals. I never saw anyone hiding food or eating like they couldn't stop. I feel so ashamed and sad about it. You know, making that food, it used to mean family and caring for my children. But now... now it feels like I've turned it into something bad. I'm really not sure about that. I haven't paid much attention to whether I'm feeling stressed or upset when it happens. Sometimes I just start eating and... well, I honestly don't know if my emotions have anything to do with it. I just haven't noticed a pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you often, or was it just a one-time thing?\n\nPatient: No, it's not just a one-time thing. It's... well, since my friend passed, it's been happening most weeks. I'd say usually once or twice a week. It mostly seems to happen on those quiet afternoons, you know, after I get back from choir practice or when I'm done with my gardening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating these comfort foods makes you feel better while you're doing it?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean... I guess I haven't really thought about whether it makes me feel better in the moment. I'm not sure. I just know how terrible I feel afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat these comfort foods as a way to deal with difficult emotions or thoughts?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. Like I said before, I just haven't paid much attention to my thoughts or feelings when it happens. I don't know if I'm trying to deal with anything... I just find myself eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens when you're trying to distract yourself from thinking about your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say for sure. I mean, I miss her terribly, of course, but... I don't know if I'm doing it to distract myself. I just haven't noticed if she's on my mind right before I start eating. Like I said, I'm just not really paying attention to what's going through my head at the time. I just end up doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating these comfort foods is related to feeling sad about your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I really don't know. I mean, it's possible, I suppose, but I just haven't made that connection. Like I was saying, I'm just not really aware of what I'm feeling or why I'm doing it when it happens. I just know that I start eating and I can't seem to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens when you're feeling sad or upset about something?\n\nPatient: I know you keep asking me about my feelings, but I honestly just don't have a better answer for you. I'm not sitting there crying or feeling mad right before I go to the kitchen. At least, not that I notice. It just... happens. I wish I could tell you it was because I was upset about something, but I really just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be eating as a way to try and feel better, even if you're not really aware of it at the time?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I mean, I guess it's possible that I'm trying to make myself feel better without realizing it, but... I just don't know. I'm not thinking about cheering myself up or anything like that. I just find myself eating. I wish I had a clearer answer for you, I really do, but I just haven't noticed if that's why I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to eat these comfort foods, like you can't control it?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's exactly it. Once I start eating those comfort foods... I try to stop. I'll even tell myself, \"Maria, that is enough.\" But my hand just goes right back to the pot. It really feels like I just can't make myself stop, no matter what I tell myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've been eating these comfort foods for so long now that it's just become a habit for you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I mean... I haven't really thought about it like it's just some old habit. I don't know if I've been doing it so long that it's just automatic now. I just know what happens when I'm standing there with the food. I couldn't say for sure if it's just a habit or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat these comfort foods even if you weren't feeling any emotions at all?\n\nPatient: I honestly couldn't tell you. I've never really stopped to think about whether I'd still eat them if I wasn't feeling anything at all. It's... well, it's just hard to say. I really don't know the answer to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to eat these comfort foods if you were in a different environment or situation?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I haven't really been in a different situation lately, so it's hard to say. I mostly just stay home. I don't know if being somewhere else would change how I feel about eating those foods.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat these comfort foods if you were alone in a completely new place?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I haven't traveled or been to a new place in years, to be honest. I just don't know how I'd react or if I'd still reach for those same foods if I was sitting in a strange room somewhere. It's just hard to picture it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you learned to eat these comfort foods as a way to cope with feelings or emotions from someone else, like your family or friends?\n\nPatient: I've never really thought about it like that. I don't know if I picked it up from anyone... my family, well, we just ate what we had. I don't remember anyone specifically eating to deal with their feelings. When I was growing up, food was just food. We sat down and ate what was on the table for supper, and that was that. I never noticed my parents or anybody else doing that when they were sad or upset.\n\nDoctor: Did someone in your life, like a family member or friend, also eat comfort foods in a way that seemed uncontrollable to them?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can recall. Like I said, food wasn't really a big emotional thing for us. We just ate our meals. I never saw anyone hiding food or eating like they couldn't stop. I really didn't. There wasn't anybody around me who ate like that when I was young. We just had our regular meals and that was it. It's become something I feel like I have to hide from the people at my church.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these comfort foods when you're feeling stressed or emotional?\n\nPatient: I... I'm really not sure about that. I haven't paid much attention to whether I'm feeling stressed or upset when it happens. Sometimes I just start eating and... well, I honestly don't know if my emotions have anything to do with it. I just haven't noticed a pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you often, or was it just a one-time thing?\n\nPatient: No, it's not just a one-time thing. It's... well, since my friend passed, it's been happening most weeks. I'd say usually once or twice a week. It mostly seems to happen on those quiet afternoons, you know, after I get back from choir practice or when I'm done with my gardening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating these comfort foods makes you feel better while you're doing it?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean... I guess I haven't really thought about whether it makes me feel better in the moment. I'm not sure. I just know how terrible I feel afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat these comfort foods as a way to deal with difficult emotions or thoughts?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. Like I said before, I just haven't paid much attention to my thoughts or feelings when it happens. I don't know if I'm trying to deal with anything... I just find myself eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens when you're trying to distract yourself from thinking about your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say for sure. I mean, I miss her terribly, of course, but... I don't know if I'm doing it to distract myself. I just haven't noticed if she's on my mind right before I start eating. Like I said, I'm just not really paying attention to what's going through my head at the time. I just end up doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating these comfort foods is related to feeling sad about your friend who passed away?\n\nPatient: I really don't know. I mean, it's possible, I suppose, but I just haven't made that connection. Like I was saying, I'm just not really aware of what I'm feeling or why I'm doing it when it happens. I just know that I start eating and I can't seem to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens when you're feeling sad or upset about something?\n\nPatient: I know you keep asking me about my feelings, but I honestly just don't have a better answer for you. I'm not sitting there crying or feeling mad right before I go to the kitchen. At least, not that I notice. It just... happens. I wish I could tell you it was because I was upset about something, but I really just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be eating as a way to try and feel better, even if you're not really aware of it at the time?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I mean, I guess it's possible that I'm trying to make myself feel better without realizing it, but... I just don't know. I'm not thinking about cheering myself up or anything like that. I just find myself eating. I wish I had a clearer answer for you, I really do, but I just haven't noticed if that's why I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to eat these comfort foods, like you can't control it?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's exactly it. Once I start eating those comfort foods... I try to stop. I'll even tell myself, \"Maria, that is enough.\" But my hand just goes right back to the pot. It really feels like I just can't make myself stop, no matter what I tell myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've been eating these comfort foods for so long now that it's just become a habit for you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I mean... I haven't really thought about it like it's just some old habit. I don't know if I've been doing it so long that it's just automatic now. I just know what happens when I'm standing there with the food. I couldn't say for sure if it's just a habit or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat these comfort foods even if you weren't feeling any emotions at all?\n\nPatient: I honestly couldn't tell you. I've never really stopped to think about whether I'd still eat them if I wasn't feeling anything at all. It's... well, it's just hard to say. I really don't know the answer to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to eat these comfort foods if you were in a different environment or situation?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I haven't really been in a different situation lately, so it's hard to say. I mostly just stay home. I don't know if being somewhere else would change how I feel about eating those foods.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat these comfort foods if you were alone in a completely new place?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I haven't traveled or been to a new place in years, to be honest. I just don't know how I'd react or if I'd still reach for those same foods if I was sitting in a strange room somewhere. It's just hard to picture it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you learned to eat these comfort foods as a way to cope with feelings or emotions from someone else, like your family or friends?\n\nPatient: I've never really thought about it like that. I don't know if I picked it up from anyone... my family, well, we just ate what we had. I don't remember anyone specifically eating to deal with their feelings. So, I guess I'm not really sure where it came from.\n\nDoctor: Did you grow up seeing family members or close relatives eat as a way to deal with their emotions?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying... no, I really didn't. When I was growing up, food was just food. We sat down and ate what was on the table for supper, and that was that. I never noticed my parents or anybody else doing that when they were sad or upset.\n\nDoctor: Did someone in your life, like a family member or friend, also eat comfort foods in a way that seemed uncontrollable to them?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can recall. Like I said, food wasn't really a big emotional thing for us. We just ate our meals. I never saw anyone hiding food or eating like they couldn't stop. It just wasn't something that happened around me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have anyone in your life who ate in a similar way when you were growing up?\n\nPatient: No, like I've been saying... I really didn't. There wasn't anybody around me who ate like that when I was young. We just had our regular meals and that was it. I don't know anyone else who did this.\n\nDoctor: Did you learn to eat comfort foods as a way to cope from someone else?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I feel like we're just going in circles now. No, I didn't learn it from anybody. Like I keep telling you, nobody in my family used food to cope with things. It wasn't something I picked up from watching my folks or my friends. It's just... well, it's just something I started doing on my own.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
